After I complete this deep circle, you’ll need four people to each man a reflective mirror on the surface. Using a series of four focused beams of reflected sunlight, you’ll melt successive layers of ice inside the circle until you reach Rodjer’s level.” Amelia was talking much faster than normal. 
“Good, that sounds reasonable to me. I’ll trust your judgment. Bring Recon back to the ship and we’ll start over right away.” Rowan said from where he stood in a small group with Laurel and Juniper. Juniper had been looking very pale and slightly shaken since she had had the risks explained to her. But she stood there watching Rowan and Laurel calmly. She shook her head in the affirmative. It was a go. 
“I’ve already recalled Recon and it’s now docked. I’m putting all my resources into manufacturing three more reflective mirrors like the one already on the surface. The mirror already on the surface seems to have been outside the area of the cave in. The remaining three mirrors should be completed by the time you arrive at Recon. I’ll explain in more detail the procedure involved in melting successive layers of ice on your way to the comet’s surface.” Amelia addressed them again in an almost awed tone. 
“Maynard, I’d very much appreciate your help on this rescue attempt. But it’s totally up to you whether you choose to accept the risk this entails. I’ll understand if you would rather not go.” Rowan spoke quickly. 
“I’d be most pleased if you would accept my limited help.” Maynard said gallantly. He didn’t understand why they were making this rescue attempt. And, because he didn’t understand, everything that was happening now was an insight into human interactions and he definitely wanted to go with them. 
“I’d be glad to go along and help, also.” Willow offered as well. 
“No. There’s no need for anyone else to risk their lives. You’re needed more right here on the ship. We don’t know yet what happened to cause the cave in that buried Rodjer. It could just as easily happen again while we’re down there. Juniper insists on going. I know for a fact that Laurel isn’t going to let me go down there without her. If anything happens to either one of us, the other one wants to be there too.” Rowan went and hugged Willow. “If anything happens to us, we need you to be here.” 
“I understand. I’ll be pulling with you from here. But be very careful.” Willow managed to add as the other four ran out the door and down the path to where Recon was docked. 

* * *

Recon was once again headed towards the comet. This journey was much different though. Amelia insisted on going over and over the low-level focused melting process that they would have to perform on the comet’s surface. They would all four have to be coordinated in their efforts so they could free Rodjer as soon as possible. Amelia had devised an intricate plan that entailed each of them melting a certain portion of the circle’s area in a rotating pattern to achieve the unified effect of causing the topmost sheet of ice to melt in such a successive way as to always be melting it in the most efficient manner. 
Actually it was just as well that they spent the short time until the arrival on the comet’s surface trying to memorize and coordinate their plans. With their minds occupied so completely, they didn’t have time to dwell on the risk of what they were doing or the results to Rodjer if they failed. 
“Should we really be doing this?” Rowan whispered to Laurel when they were off by themselves for a brief moment. 
“You know as well as I do that Juniper would’ve come to the surface herself and at that probably with only a shovel to try to dig Rodjer out. We couldn’t let her attempt it alone. She would’ve failed without more people to help.” Laurel smiled a small smile at Rowan. “You’ve known for the last seventy-five years that I’d rather be anywhere with you than be anywhere without you. And that includes the surface of a giant snowball hurtling through space.” Laurel reached up and kissed Rowan’s cheek that was so close to her own. “Besides if it comes to that, I’d rather die with you than attempt to live without you.” She smiled up into his eyes. “So . . . I’m in for the duration. Besides we’ll do it.” 
“I know you’re right. Amelia has done her best to come up with a good plan. I know Amelia’s doing all she can to protect us down there. By having all four of us distributed around in a circle, there’ll be less weight and movement to cause another cave in. We’ll just have to trust Amelia and hope we can carry it off in time for Rodjer. We’ve got to.” Rowan tried to sound hopeful. 
Laurel and Rowan glanced at Juniper. Juniper was totally absorbed. She had put herself so completely into understanding and learning her part in the rescue plan that she was no longer pale. 
“Juniper.” For a moment all four of the people in Recon froze. 
“Juniper.” Rodjer’s calm quiet voice repeated her name a second time before Juniper snapped out of it and answered. 
“Rodjer, are you OK?” Juniper said faintly, barely above a whisper. 
“I’m fine, Juniper. I’m an AI. You shouldn’t be risking your life to get me out. This circumstance is one that I’ve always known could happen. I’m prepared for it. I’m still transmitting to Amelia. Hopefully I’ll complete my Dump. You’ll be able to build another AI and include whatever portion of my Dump you’d like to use. I’d hope you would create me again, but that’s your choice. You don’t need to take this risk of trying to rescue me.” Rodjer’s voice sounded odd, like it was coming from a long way off. “If there are portions of my hardware that it’s important for you to retrieve, Maynard could dig them out. But even that would present an unnecessary risk to him.” 
“No!” Juniper spoke with such force that the others gathered in Recon with her looked at her with startled expressions. “I will not let you die!” 
“Juniper, you don’t understand. I can’t die as humans do. I’ve never been alive as humans are.” Rodjer spoke as slowly and clearly as he could. 
“You are, too. You’re as alive as I am.” Juniper said determinedly. “If I don’t get you out in time, you’ll cease to exist in the same form as I know you. And I refuse to let that happen. What I could resurrect from your memory/thought systems stored with Amelia and from bits and pieces that look like you . . . wouldn’t be you.” 
“It wouldn’t be the Rodjer that knows how to boogie board.” Juniper continued in an even more determined voice. “Not the Rodjer that knows how to feel when the right wave comes along.” 
And when Juniper said that, Rodjer knew that there was nothing he could say that would change her mind. From where Rodjer lay trapped deep beneath the surface of the comet, he knew without a doubt that there was nothing he could say that would discourage Juniper from attempting to rescue him. He didn’t understand why she was risking so much to rescue him. But he understood that she was going to try and nothing would change her mind. 
As he lay motionless under the ice, Rodjer remembered the wave he and Juniper had caught together. He remembered the yell that had welled up inside him. He could almost grasp the reasoning behind why Juniper was risking what she was. But it still eluded him. He almost wished she would succeed in her rescue, just so he could get a chance to figure it out. He knew he almost had it. 
If it weren’t for the danger the rescue plan posed for Juniper and the others, a part of him could almost be glad she was trying. It would be very nice to experience that yell welling up inside of him again and to understand where it came from. 
So Rodjer gave up trying to persuade her. Always efficient, he recognized when it was more energy efficient not to try. He just hoped that Juniper didn’t fall through the surface of the comet while trying to rescue him and end up dying in the cold beside him.
“Oh . . . I know the real reason you’re coming to the comet. You didn’t get to come over with me this morning . . . so you’re just grasping at any old excuse that comes along . . . just so you can get a chance to see a comet up close and personal.” Rodjer let a touch of a smile come into his voice. “You’re just jealous. You don’t want me to have all the fun.” 
A smile slowly spread across Juniper’s face as a tear slipped slowly down her cheek. She knew Rodjer had given up trying to change her mind. He was teasing her now to help her believe that she just might get him out. 
“Sure . . . I’m jealous.” Juniper said, her voice gaining strength. “I don’t want you to be the only one who gets famous for writing the first close-up report on a comet. You just want to hog all the glory . . . and I’m not going to let you get away with it.” 
Laurel caught Rowan’s eye and they exchanged a knowing smile. They knew that there’s an elusive something that some couples have and others do not, and that Juniper and Rodjer were showing the first signs of having it. The thought warmed their hearts. Juniper had a chance of having what they had enjoyed all their lives. And as risky as this rescue effort was, they were glad they were making it. 
Maynard sat in front of the display screen. To all apparent appearances, his little furry body was intent on Amelia’s displays as she ran through the plan once again. But curiously Maynard wasn’t paying the least bit of attention to the screen. He was listening and recording all the nuances of what had just happened. He didn’t understand it all, but he recognized that something had happened that didn’t happen often and that it was an important insight into human relationships. Later with Amelia he would try to figure out what had happened and why. 
“Rodjer, I want you to listen to me closely. I’m going to be busy for the next few hours.” Juniper was speaking again as she stood up and walked to the front of Recon where the comet was displayed in all its frigid beauty in the observation window. 
“I want you to help me rescue you. I won’t take ‘No’ for an answer. You know as well as I do that to argue with me won’t accomplish a thing. So please listen carefully. I need to know while I’m trying to uncover layer after layer of ice to get to you, that you haven’t given up. I want you to keep transmitting your Dump to Amelia, but I also want a portion of you to do two important tasks for me. Are you listening?” Juniper’s voice was very determined. 
“Yes. Juniper. I’m listening.” Rodjer answered her. 
“Good. Now this is what I want you to do. Each of the two tasks involves you holding a picture in your mind. In the first picture, you’re to picture my hand covering the area of your body where your vital centers are. I want you to concentrate and gradually picture my hand getting warmer and warmer. Eventually you’ll picture my hand as a scorching hot chunk of coal.” Juniper paused for a moment.
“In the second picture, bit by bit I want you to create a picture of a roaring fire. In front of that fire, I want you to picture you and me drinking great big mugs of hot chocolate. I want you to see beads of sweat forming on my temples. I want you to see beads of sweat making the hairs at the nape of my neck hot and moist. I want you to picture you and me in front of that fire until you can see every single detail. Every hot breath I take . . . every bead of sweat that forms on my body . . . every crackle and pop of that fire. Do you hear me? I want all the details when we get back to the ship. And if you can’t describe that scene in every little detail . . . I’ll be real pissed! You hear me? Now concentrate and picture my hand on you!” Juniper was yelling by the time she finished. 
“Yes.” Rodjer replied. He didn’t say anything else. But that was enough. It had to be enough because Recon was touching down and she had a lot of work to do.
 

## Chapter 2 — JASMINE AND RODJER




 	As soon as Recon landed, Rowan powered on Recon’s external gravity field and then powered down Recon. Quickly and silently the three humans stripped and donned their spacesuits. Although the spacesuits appeared to be only paper thin, the suits were a marvel of nanotechnology. Many layers of microscopic nanotechnology machines covered each of their bodies now. The suits could do everything old-time spacesuits did, plus a lot more. Nanotechnology machines made intricate adjustments to control the temperature inside the suits, to gave objects hitting the outside of the suits less force, and to gave each motion of a human’s gloved hand as much force as they required. 
When the four rescuers emerged into the storm of the comet’s surface, Amelia was simultaneously in contact with all of them individually and each of the rescuers was in contact with Amelia and each other. They were as ready as Amelia could make them.
Amelia had never before attempted to use humans in quite this fashion, using them as extensions of herself. She would be calling on the humans to instantaneously do as she asked. She had been assured that each of them would carry out her instructions as quickly and as efficiently as they were physically capable of doing.
Amid the swirling bits and pieces of ice and mist, Amelia calmly and efficiently guided the rescuers through the steps they needed to perform. Once again Amelia had set Recon down very close to where they needed to be. She had already burned a large circle into the comet’s surface while Recon was transporting the rescuers to the comet. It was only a matter of a few minutes until the four mirrors were stationed at their respective spots around the circle. 
Not long after Recon landed Amelia began to send the reflected beams of light down to the mirrors controlled on the surface. Maynard followed Amelia’s instructions quickly, making himself a direct link with her. The three humans tried their best to make themselves into extensions of Amelia as well. Quickly they precisely carried out each of her swiftly changing orders. Only a small part of the rescuers’ awareness was focused on how much the ice was melting inside the circle. 
Gradually the inside of the circle began to smooth out and appear to slowly sink into the comet. Since Maynard’s body didn’t tire and he was much faster at receiving her instructions, Amelia had Maynard carry out the majority of the work. She had to be very careful not to tire the humans too much. As willing as the humans were to do the utmost they could, their bodies could only do things so fast and so long. The safety and health of the humans was also an important concern of Amelia’s. Constantly monitoring the comet, Amelia strove to detect any change in the surface of the comet. She hoped she would be able to warn the rescuers before another cave in occurred. 
It seemed to the diligently concentrating rescuers that they had only been following Amelia’s orders for a short time when she called a halt. It was only when they stopped that they realized how exhausted they were and how much the ice in the center of the circle had melted. Amelia had Maynard continue just a few minutes more on his own. She needed the last movements on Maynard’s part to be very delicate and precise, so Rodjer wouldn’t be baked in their attempt to rescue him. 
During the last few moments, Amelia reflected a much more powerful beam down to Maynard’s mirror and he just held a line positioned well above the level of the ice. In this way, Amelia just used the heat radiating off the beam and not the beam itself to melt the last bit of ice away. 
Juniper and Laurel and Rowan stood in their designated spots around the circle and watched silently as Maynard carried out Amelia’s last instructions. They had been instructed not to move. Amelia didn’t want them to stand in one spot together or move around too much. The danger of another cave in was too great. So they stood silently and watched as, seemingly by magic, a shape took form in the center of the circle. Before it had fully sunk in that they were finished, there was Rodjer lying in the center of the circle. 
“Rodjer! Rodjer!” Juniper began yelling. She forgot all about Amelia’s instructions and ran to where he lay. 
“Juniper, stand still and be quiet.” It was a few moments before Amelia could get Juniper’s attention. “Rodjer is fine. We’ve reached him in time.” 
“Rodjer . . . are you OK?” Juniper was bending over his still form, frantically looking for some sign of life. 
“Juniper, he can’t answer you now . . . not by speaking. I can transmit his words to you.” Amelia was getting exasperated. “Juniper, you must leave Rodjer and walk out of the circle.” She waited until Juniper began to comply with her words. 
“Rowan, I want you and Maynard to slowly and carefully walk to Rodjer and pick him up. I don’t want too much weight or movement in one spot. Juniper . . . if you go and stand quietly by Recon, I’ll transmit Rodjer’s voice to you while Maynard and Rowan move him. We need to get him to Recon as soon as possible.” 
Reluctantly Juniper continued to move out of the circle. She and Laurel, walking as if on egg shells, went to stand by Recon. They watched as Maynard and Rowan slowly and carefully lifted the nearly frozen Rodjer and carried him towards Recon. 
“Juniper.” Rodjer’s voice came to everyone over their linkup with Amelia.
“Rodjer, are you OK?” Juniper asked breathlessly. 
“Yes, I’m fine. Although it does seem that I’ll require a few minutes in Lab to allow the nanotechnology machines time to repair me a bit. I’ve discontinued my Dump process though . . . since it appears that you did manage to reach me in time.” Rodjer’s voice filled their helmets. 
The conversation was interrupted. Maynard and Rowan, using the utmost care, were attempting to load Rodjer into Recon. In the end it took Laurel and Juniper’s help as well to accomplish the task. They didn’t want to injure Rodjer’s semi-frozen body any more than it already was. 
Amelia kept Recon hovered just barely off the comet’s surface as Maynard quickly proceeded out through the hatch again. 
“Where’s Maynard going?” Rowan asked as he began to remove his spacesuit.
“While we’re here, we may as well set up the original mirror designed to focus the jet of steam.” Amelia answered him. 
“Right, we still have to capture the comet.” Rowan said as he got Laurel’s help to extract his arm from the stubborn sleeve of his spacesuit. “But shouldn’t we get Rodjer up to Lab as soon as possible?” 
“The delay will only be very short. Rodjer’s condition will no longer deteriorate, now that he’s inside Recon. His temperature is rising and a short delay in getting him to Lab won’t harm him in any way.” Amelia answered and, true to her word, it was only a few minutes later that Maynard returned and Recon lifted off. 

* * *

Lab was rather crowded. Maynard and Rowan, with Laurel and Juniper doing all they could to help, lifted Rodjer’s semi-thawed form onto the nearest worktable. Immediately Rowan and Maynard began to hook Rodjer up to the Rejuv Machine. Willow moved closer to get a better look, the baby dinosaur scurrying under everyone’s feet. Willow tried her best to keep Keeko corralled off to one side, but she was only marginally successful. Keeko tended to wander off and get into everything. Finally, with a sigh and a shrug, Willow gave up completely and joined the crowd that pressed in at all sides around Rodjer. 
“Are you OK?” Juniper kept asking Rodjer, and Amelia kept repeating that Rodjer was fine. That soon he’d be able to talk. 
“What happened? Are you OK? Can we do anything to help?” The questions came thick and fast. Finally Amelia broke in above the racket. 
“Everyone . . . may I have your attention?” While waiting patiently for everyone to settle down, Amelia was already starting to direct her troops of nanotechnology machines in their repair work. 
“Good.” Amelia continued when everyone at last was waiting to listen to her. “I’ve already begun to repair the damage done to Rodjer. In just a few minutes he’ll be able to answer all your questions. I would suggest that until then everyone relax and stand back and let him have some room.” 
“You’re right.” Rowan spoke up amid the many apologies that resounded towards Rodjer. They all disbursed to perch on various workstation chairs, a few lucky ones managing to commandeer the Lab couch. 
Juniper remained beside Rodjer, not once looking anywhere else or stopping the endless “Are you OK?” phrase she kept repeating over and over. But now she kept her voice down to a low murmur.
“Juniper . . . I’m OK.” A few minutes later Rodjer was finally able to speak for himself. 
“Oh! I’m so glad! Are you sure? Are you hurt?” Juniper’s words fairly tripped over each other in their rush to get spoken. 
“It’s OK. I’m OK.” Rodjer smiled at Juniper. “I’m not hurt. Amelia and her army are just now finishing the last repairs. I’ll be good as new.” 
“Oh! I’m so glad! Is there anything you need? Is there anything I can get you?” Juniper urgently asked wanting to do anything she could for Rodjer. 
“Slow down. I don’t need you to do anything for me. But you must calm down and relax.” Rodjer looked at her with concern in his eyes. “You look exhausted.” 
“Well . . . now that you mention it . . . I am a bit tired.” Juniper slowly pulled herself up to perch on the table beside him. “I believe I’ll just sit here and wait. I want to be sure you’re OK.” 
“That’s a good idea. You just sit still and stay quiet. As soon as I’m done here, you need to go home and take a nap.” Rodjer lay still for a few minutes longer, and then he slowly began to unfasten himself from the Rejuv Machine. “There . . . I’m all repaired . . . good as new.” 
“Oh, good.” Juniper visibly breathed a huge sigh of relief as Rodjer climbed down from the worktable and turned to help her down.
“How are you doing?” Laurel spoke as she and Rowan came over to ask after Rodjer’s well being. And along with them, Willow and Maynard joined the group around Rodjer. 
“I’m fine. Really . . . everyone . . . I’m as good as new.” Rodjer shook his head. “You should all go home and rest up a bit. I think this ordeal has taken more out of you all than it has out of me. Or . . . at least my damage was more quickly repaired.” 
A strange expression filled Rodjer’s eyes. He seemed about to speak, and then he stopped. Then he started again. 
“I don’t know quite what to say . . . to all of you. I’m not sure why you risked so much to rescue me. You really shouldn’t have done that. The risk was much too great for the benefit received. But needless to say . . . thanks to you all . . . I’m back on the ship, all in one piece and safe and sound.” Rodjer bowed graciously, and then gave a slight laugh. “Thanks for all the efforts you expended on my behalf, but don’t ever pull a crazy stunt like that again. I’m imminently replaceable.” When Rodjer finished speaking he was looking significantly in Juniper’s direction. Juniper just smiled back at him, ignoring his implied unhappiness at the risk she’d taken. 
“Do you know what caused the comet’s surface to collapse like that?” Willow asked. 
“No. I’m sure you know as much or more than I do. I was standing there sinking the anchor and detaching the drill, when suddenly I was falling. Maybe Amelia has some answers for us.” Rodjer spoke in answer. 
“I’m in the process of studying the comet to look for possible causes behind the surface collapse in that area. So far I’ve detected nothing that points to a reasonable explanation of why that surface area would collapse like it did. I’ll keep searching though.” Amelia answered their unasked question.
“Well . . . I’m going to kidnap Rowan and we’re going to have a brief siesta. I know to you the benefit of the risk we took was not high enough, but to us you were and are . . . well worth the risk.” Laurel smiled at Rodjer. “But just the same, I’m glad we’re all back safe and sound. And I don’t know about the rest of you, but I’m just beginning to realize just how exhausted I am. This rescue business is quite the workout.” Laurel put her arm around Rowan and began urging him towards the door. She didn’t have to urge much. 
“Amelia, you may as well begin beaming down to the comet. We might as well start that comet towards its new orbit.” Rowan spoke just before he disappeared out the door. Amelia answered in the affirmative, but Rowan and Laurel had already left. Walking slowly with their arms around each other, they were already on their way towards their dome.
“Well, I guess that’s that.” Willow gave Rodjer a hug. “I’m glad you’re back to normal. It was mighty scary there for a while.” She glanced around to locate Keeko. “I think I’ll just take this furry bunch of energy to the meadow. I can lie in the shade by the waterfall and catch a few winks while she explores the meadow.” Willow bent down and scooped up Keeko. 
“You wouldn’t think I’d be tired. I haven’t done anything all day. But I guess I did expend a huge amount of nervous energy.” Willow smiled at Rodjer and Juniper. “I’m going to go see if I can get Keeko to entertain herself, so I can steal some shut eye.”
Maynard was recruited by Amelia to help her run some simulations in the search for the answer to what caused the cave in. Amelia had picked the Recon landing spot and the mirror anchoring spot especially for their apparently safe surface area. She didn’t like the fact that something had gone wrong with her choice. She wanted to find the answer to why her exploratory data hadn’t shown the likelihood of a cave in at that location. 
Rodjer and Juniper looked around. Suddenly Lab was virtually empty. Seated at one of the workstations, Maynard was helping Amelia. Everyone else was gone, disappearing into the quiet afternoon. 
“Well . . . let’s get you home. You’re going to be collapsing on your feet any minute.” Rodjer said as he steered Juniper towards the door. He was beginning to be concerned that she may have over done it in her act of rescuing him. 
For once Juniper didn’t argue with him. She just turned and walked towards the door. As they walked down the path that led towards her dome, Juniper’s legs began to move slower and slower. 
“Rodjer . . . I think I’m more tired than I thought. I don’t believe . . . I’ve ever had a workout . . . like that before. I’m so glad . . . we got to you in time. I just couldn’t bear it if . . .” Her voice trailed off. 
Luckily for her, Rodjer had been watching her very closely. He recognized the signs immediately. Being fast on his feet, he managed to scoop her up into his arms before she even realized she was falling. Held in his arms, she promptly went to sleep. Her head bounced a little where it rested against his chest and Rodjer slowed his steps so his gait would be more even. There was no longer any need to hurry towards her dome. Juniper was already resting. 
Walking slowly along the path, Rodjer took in the scent of the flowers growing nearby, the fragrance of the trees with their beautiful blossoms, and the smell of Juniper so close to him. Even though she was a bit sweaty and grimy from her work on his behalf, a faint whiff of her perfume found its way to his nose. Rodjer smiled. Now that the risk to his rescuers was over, he allowed himself the knowledge that he was very glad they had been successful. 

* * *

Carefully and ever so gently, Rodjer placed Juniper on the soft cushions at the bottom of the window seat. Outside the window, the sight and sounds of the beach filled the late afternoon silence. Moving to the head of the giant window seat that Juniper used as her bed, he pulled down the covers. 
Juniper stirred as Rodjer was about to lift her and place her beneath the covers.
“No . . . I’m filthy. I need . . . a bath. I’ve got to get all this grime . . . off . . . me.” Juniper mumbled her eyes still closed. 
“OK, I’ll go run you a nice tub full of hot water.” Rodjer moved towards the bathroom. “But I’d better stay with you. In your present condition . . . you’re liable to go completely to sleep and sink to the bottom.” 
Rodjer called up a tub full of steamy water, asking that some of Juniper’s bubble bath be added as well. Juniper wasn’t much help when it came to getting her out of her clothes. Her feeble attempts to help weren’t needed because he soon had her stripped and soaking in the fragrant bubbles. He stayed and helped her scrub the biggest majority of the grime off. He let her soak for a while, and then he hauled her out and into bed. 
Even as Juniper lay safe and snug and clean and warm in her bed with the covers pulled up to her chin, Rodjer still didn’t leave. Usually when she was asleep, he would go and work on one of the various projects he always seemed to be involved in. He would work on his project until she woke up and needed him for something again. This time though, he stayed. 
Rodjer used the tub to wash himself off. He seemed to have collected a bit of grime himself in his adventure on the comet’s surface. He then got a clean pair of shorts and a t-shirt from the supply Juniper always kept for him. He settled himself comfortably at the bottom of the huge window seat in which she slept. 
Watching Juniper’s chest raise and lower the covers with each slight breath she took, Rodjer was amazed again at how fragile human life was. Yet Juniper had risked this fragile human life of hers, just to take the unnecessary risk of rescuing him. He wondered why. 
Now wondering why Juniper did something was not new to Rodjer. He was often puzzled by what she did and why. Usually he would ask a few questions, reason a while, and come up with a viable explanation. But this latest stunt she had pulled was a doozy. She had succeeded. But she could just as easily have died for her efforts, more easily probably. 
So Rodjer sat and wondered. When he got the chance, he would ask Juniper to explain why. But he couldn’t think of one conceivable reason she could’ve had, to explain the risk she had taken. Listening to the birds outside the window, the sound of the nearby waves, and the sound of her soft breathing, he wondered as the late afternoon hours slid into night.

* * *

“How long have you been sitting there?” Were the first words out of Juniper’s mouth when she woke up. 
“Four hours, 9 minutes, and 30 seconds . . . make that 32 seconds.” Rodjer smiled at her. 
“What time is it?” Juniper said as she sat up. 
“Eight fifteen . . . give or take a minute or two.” Rodjer answered quietly. “You look like you feel much better now.” 
Juniper realized about then that she was naked. Of course Rodjer had seen her naked many times. Everyone always went swimming nude. There was just no other comfortable way to swim in ocean surf and sand. But she hadn’t found herself in quite this position before. Naked in bed, facing Rodjer, with her chest exposed. Should she cover up or forget her nakedness. She decided to forget it. 
“Thanks . . . I do feel better. I must have pretty much just collapsed on you.” Juniper spoke as she got up and went to the Food Dispenser for a drink of water. 
“Actually . . . you would’ve collapsed on the path about two steps from Lab. But I caught you before you hit the ground.” Rodjer grinned at Juniper. 
Even though he’d seen her many times swimming without a stitch on, this was the longest he’d had an unobstructed view of her. And he wasn’t at all surprised to find he was enjoying it. He had always known he enjoyed looking at beauty, wherever he happened to find it. 
“Well . . . I guess I should thank you for being such a gentleman. Carrying me here . . . and I seem to remember a tub full of warm bubbles.” Juniper gazed at Rodjer questioningly. “There’s nothing else I should be remembering . . . is there?” 
“I just dried you off a bit . . . and tucked you in bed. A gentleman is duty bound to his honor . . . you know.” Rodjer grinned, a bit of a twinkle in his eye. “Believe me . . . if there was more for you to remember . . . you’d remember it well . . . and with pleasure, too.” He couldn’t resist adding. 
Juniper looked closely at Rodjer over the glass of water that she was slowly sipping. Was he teasing her or was he partly serious? 
“Oh . . . I believe you. I’ve read all those reports on AIs. I figure that even if one cuts the results in half . . . do to exaggerations . . .” Juniper grinned at Rodjer. “The results are still pretty impressive.” 
“You betcha. We aim to please.” Rodjer laughed out loud at his own joke, watching Juniper’s reaction. 
In their past conversations they had touched on this subject many times before. But those conversations had occurred in Lab as they read over a new release from the Net. Joking and teasing came easy then. But now in this circumstance, he didn’t want to make her uncomfortable. 
Juniper was becoming a bit flustered, but she was determined not to let Rodjer know it. 
“I’ll bet you do.” Was all she managed to answer. 
“Well . . . I guess I’ll be going. Amelia can probably use my help to track down the cause of the cave in.” Rodjer was not sure how to leave. Usually he said “Is there anything more I can do for you?” before he left, to make sure Juniper didn’t need him for anything. But in this instance Rodjer wasn’t sure he should say that. 
“Not so fast, buster!” Juniper jumped up and barred Rodjer’s exit path to the door. 
“What?” For once Juniper had managed to genuinely take him by surprise. 
“Your report . . . I want to hear your report.” Juniper was definitely proud of herself. It was rare that she was able to catch Rodjer off guard. 
“My report?” Rodjer was surprised at himself. He never forgot anything. 
“Yes . . . your report. But not here. I think you should report in an appropriate place. Amelia?” Juniper purposefully neglected to enlighten Rodjer any further. 
“Yes, Juniper.” Amelia always had a link to each dome, no matter how busy she was elsewhere. 
“Is someone occupying the Beach Shack?” Juniper continued to speak to Amelia, ignoring Rodjer completely. 
“No. No one is there and no one has requested use of it.” Amelia answered her. The Beach Shack was a communal dome that was used by anyone who wanted to use it when it wasn’t otherwise occupied. 
“Good, I’d like to request it for tonight. And could you start a fire in the fireplace. I’ll be there in a few minutes.” Juniper had a small smile growing in her eyes. 
“Yes, I’ll do as you’ve requested. Anything else?” Amelia asked. 
“No, that’s all . . . except that I should probably tell you that Rodjer won’t be available to help you for a while. But I’m sure you’ll get along fine without him. Thank you.”
“Yes. Maynard is helping me and I can manage quite well without Rodjer. Thank you for your concern.” Amelia signed off. 
“Well . . . what shall I wear?” Juniper continued to ignore Rodjer. “Oh well . . . a blanket will do fine.” Juniper said as she grabbed a blanket off her bed and made for the door. “Aren’t you coming?” She called back to Rodjer as she disappeared through the door. 
Juniper had definitely regained her energy resources. Rodjer had to move quite fast to catch up to her. 
“Juniper, what report?” Rodjer spoke as soon as he reached Juniper’s side. 
“You remember when you were trapped under the icy rubble of the comet’s surface, you promised to make a picture for me. Well, now I want you to tell me all the details . . . well not now . . . but as soon as we get to the Beach Shack.” Juniper let the edges of the blanket she clutched around her shoulders slide a little. “I thought it would be more fun to hear your report in front of a roaring fire. That way . . . I can see if your details are correct.” She teased. 
“Oh . . . that report. Are you serious? You want me to tell you the picture I came up with?” Rodjer couldn’t imagine why. 
“Of course . . . I want to hear every last detail. But first, let me get settled in front of the fire. And we’ll need large mugs of hot chocolate, and then you can start reporting.” Juniper said in as determined a voice as she could muster. 
“OK . . . if that’s what you want . . . I’ll report, of course. But I’m not even sure why you wanted me to detail that picture in the first place.” Rodjer decided to chalk this up to another of Juniper’s actions that would need some explaining before he’d see the reasoning behind it. 
Just as the last of their beautiful sun’s rays were sinking into their horizon, the Beach Shack came into view. Amelia had even been thoughtful enough to turn the exterior light on. They circled past the Beach Shack storage room, where they usually stored their clothes and anything else they didn’t want to take down to the beach. And they passed by the outside showers, which were handy for a quick rinse off after swimming. Then Juniper rounded the corner and entered the Beach Shack dome. 
A lovely fire was crackling in the fireplace. Its subdued bursts of light lit the room in a subtle way, making the two workstations and the bed mere shadowy structures. Juniper went to the bearskin rug which lay in front of the fireplace. It was a fundamental part of the ambiance of the Beach Shack. She plopped down. 
“Would you be so kind as to get us each a giant mug of hot chocolate . . . about this big?” Juniper used both hands to encircle an imaginary mug. 
“Sure.” Rodjer answered quietly. He got two giant mugs of hot chocolate from the Food Dispenser and asked for lots of whipped cream to be added on top, knowing Juniper liked it that way as did he.
With some difficulty Rodjer arrived back at the bearskin rug on which Juniper sat, without having spilled a drop. No small feat with the large quantities of whipped cream threatening to cascade off the edges of the mugs. Carefully he handed Juniper’s to her and settled himself on the bearskin rug beside her. 
“So . . . let’s hear that report of yours.” Juniper said while licking up bits of whipped cream. “And . . . it had better be good . . . or remember . . . I’m going to be really pissed.” She added while grinning up into Rodjer’s face. 
The sight nearly took her breath away, tanned good looks with beach bum blonde hair that hung shaggily to his shoulders. Damn, she’d done a good job creating his looks. Maybe too good a job, Juniper thought as she felt her heart go “thud”. 
“My report . . . OK . . . here goes . . . just as you so forcefully instructed.” Rodjer smiled teasingly at Juniper. “I pictured a fire burning in a fireplace . . . not unlike the fire you see in front of you now.” He turned to look at the sizzling blaze that shone its reflected light on their faces. “The fire I pictured was about twice as big as this one, with deep red coals. The kind of coals that only exist after a fire has been burning a long time. The fire had bits of yellow flame shooting up with the merest hint of blue flame bursting forth now and again.” 
“All right . . . I think I have the fire you pictured in mind.” Juniper broke in. “Now . . . go on with the rest of your picture.”
“Well . . . I pictured you and I in front of the fire . . . just like you requested. And we held mugs of hot chocolate . . . not unlike the ones we’re holding now.” Rodjer paused to lick some whipped cream that was melting and escaping over the edge of his mug. 
While he was licking up the bits of cream, Juniper tried desperately to keep her mind on his report. Her thoughts seemed to skitter off at the oddest moments to contemplate things she couldn’t afford to consider, not now anyway.  
“Although, I don’t think I pictured the mugs as quite this big and I seem to have forgotten the whip cream in my picture.” Rodjer continued his report. “Anyway . . . we’re in front of this crackling fire that’s radiating heat at us. We’re drinking our hot chocolate, minus the whip cream . . . and then I began to attempt to picture the beads of sweat forming at your temples.” Rodjer turned from the fire and grinned at Juniper. “Actually . . . I got quite good at picturing beads of perspiration forming all over your body . . . at your temples . . . at the nape of your neck, where the tiny short hairs curl so tightly underneath the longer ones.” Rodjer continued speaking slowly.
“I saw each bead of moisture slowly form between your breasts . . . as your chest rose and fell with each breath you took.” Rodjer had been looking at Juniper, a slight smile pulling gently at the corners of his mouth. But now his gaze was definitely sliding down. 
Juniper watched as Rodjer’s eyes left her face and began to slide, sinking slowly down. It was definitely getting a bit warm in the room, more than a bit. But for some reason Juniper didn’t dare let the blanket draped around her shoulders fall any lower. 
The gaze from Rodjer’s smoldering blue eyes climbed slowly from where he had been watching the place where the two folds of the blanket came together just above Juniper’s breasts. His gaze came at last to rest on her face once more as he continued his report. 
“In my attempt to follow your orders . . . I pictured carefully each delicate bead of moisture as they sprang from the place between your breasts . . . and slowly slid down to join the beads that formed in the area just below your breasts. I pictured the crystal clear tiny streams slip and slide . . . to gently flow over each of your ribs . . . and continue slowly down towards your belly. I pictured a few tiny streams reach your belly and gradually fill the dimple of your belly button to overflowing. I pictured the overflow of hot moisture . . . slowly making its way . . . gently down . . .” 
“Ah . . . well . . . I think I have the picture.” Juniper managed to stammer out.
“Are you sure . . . you don’t want more details? I still have much more of my picture that I haven’t gotten to yet. I’m sure you’ll want to hear all the little details I was able to come up with.” Rodjer’s eyes began to twinkle just a bit. “I know you want to hear every . . . last . . . detail. You know . . . I was buried under that ice for quite a while. I was able to make my picture . . . very detailed. I’m sure you wouldn’t want to miss any of the minute details I came up with. They really are quite impressive, I think.” 
“Well . . . I’m . . . sure . . . you did a very good job.” Juniper suddenly found herself jumping up to put her hot chocolate mug in the Recycle Slot. “I think I’ve heard enough . . . to get the general idea that you did as I asked.” 
Juniper was not quite sure what to do. She was hot and wanted very much to remove the blanket from around her shoulders. She was burning up, but she had nothing else to put on. And she wasn’t at all sure she wanted to be naked in front of Rodjer just now. 
“Well . . . if you’re sure you’ve heard enough.” Rodjer watched as Juniper began to pace back and forth in the shadows by the Food Dispenser. “I really don’t mind giving you all the details. I had plenty of time to come up with lots of details . . . all expanding on the general idea I’ve already reported to you.” 
“Oh . . . I’m sure you were able to make a very detailed picture. But right now . . . I believe I can get the general idea of the picture from what you’ve already said. And I’ll just trust that you were able to make it very detailed.” Juniper turned her back on Rodjer and tried to wave the blanket open a bit to let some cooler air in. “More detailed than I think I want to hear.” She finished under her breath. 
“Well . . . if you don’t want to hear any more of my report . . . maybe you could tell me why you requested that I form that picture in my mind.” Rodjer said while placing his empty mug next to him on the floor. 
“I wanted to . . .” Juniper began to speak as she turned around, but then she suddenly stopped. Rodjer had taken his shirt off. He was sprawled on the bearskin rug and looked so gorgeous she forgot what she was saying for a moment. 
“We could turn the fire down, if you want. It’s getting a bit hot in here. Aren’t you hot . . . in that blanket?” Rodjer asked, while watching her carefully. 
“I . . . yes . . . I am . . . rather hot. All that hot chocolate . . . I guess.” Juniper spoke as she made her way towards where Rodjer sat. 
“Well . . . why don’t I get you something cool to drink? You must be burning up inside that blanket.” Rodjer sprang to his feet and made his way to the Food Dispenser. 
“Yes . . . as a matter of fact . . . I’ll just sit here. Beach Shack, please turn down the fire. Thank you.” Juniper turned her back slightly towards where Rodjer was at the Food Dispenser and lowered the blanket to her waist. The cooler air felt marvelous.
“Here.” Rodjer said as he held out a large glass of soda that was virtually brimming with ice cubes. 
Juniper whirled around to take what Rodjer offered and found herself facing him, watching as his eyes took in the blanket where it lay in her lap. 
“Thanks.” Juniper spoke as she reached out as nonchalantly as she could for the cool drink. 
“Junie.” Rodjer’s voice sounded a bit strange. She raised her gaze from the drink she was reaching for to look at his face. 
“I think . . . I’d better go.” Rodjer handed Juniper the drink and walked over to the window that faced the ocean. 
Rodjer watched the waves as they rose and fell on the lonely stretch of beach. The moonlight made the waves sparkle and the foam glisten. He sat down on the bed, which was the only piece of furniture on that side of the dome. He watched the waves and wondered what the hell he should say. 
“I’d better go.” Was all he managed to come up with. 
“Why?” Juniper said from his elbow. 
Rodjer turned, surprised that Juniper had joined him on the bed and was also looking out at the ocean. He did notice that she clutched the blanket around her shoulders once again. 
“Junie . . . I don’t know what to say.” Rodjer looked at Juniper carefully. “I seem to be suddenly caught between wanting to say something . . . and not being able to say it . . . because of the chance that it will upset you.” He paused. “I really think . . . I should just leave.” 
“Well . . . I think you should just say . . . what it is you want to say.” Juniper spoke very softly. “I’m not a child, you know.” 
“Of course . . . I know that.” Rodjer sounded frustrated. “A person would have to be blind . . . not to know that.” He paused. “I know . . . that’s not what you meant.” 
“I find myself in an uncomfortable position.” Rodjer struggled on as he got up and began to pace back and forth, occasionally pausing to look out at the waves. “I’ve been aware of this position on various occasions before now . . . but never to this extent.” He paused in front of Juniper. “I’m your creation. You purchased, assembled, and programmed me.” 
“I know.” Juniper reached out and lightly touched Rodjer’s hand. “Laurel has given me bits of advice over the years. One of her most important pieces of advice was not to play games when dealing with other people on a personal level. She said it was important to talk and talk and talk and then talk some more. And . . . that it was important to be honest . . . to be honest with yourself . . . and with the other person.” Juniper looked up into Rodjer’s face and managed to produce a bit of a smile. “It takes guts. It’s easy to say . . . but very hard to do.” Juniper slowly stood up to face Rodjer. 
“We need to talk . . . we probably should have talked about it a long time ago. But . . . I’m not as brave a person as I’d like to think I am.” She laughed a very small laugh. “I can risk my life and my parents’ lives . . . rescuing you on the comet’s surface . . . but when it comes to really talking to you . . . I find it very difficult.” Juniper continued.
“So . . . here’s what we’ll do. I’m going to take this hot blanket off . . . and get cool.” Juniper spoke quietly, and she was about to do just that when Rodjer’s hands on her hands stopped her. 
“I don’t think that would be a good idea. If you take that blanket off . . . I’m going to have one split second in which to decide what to do . . . run out of here . . . or do my utmost to convince you that AIs can be fun playmates. You and Rowan have been very generous to provide me with all the latest advancements. And at this moment . . . here and now . . . I’m having a difficult time not putting all of my persuasive talents to use . . . getting you horizontal on that bearskin rug. So please . . . leave the blanket on . . . at least until I can get out of here.” Rodjer started to remove his hands from hers and turn towards the door. 
“Not so fast.” Juniper’s hands stopped him. “I’m not going to make it that easy for you.” 
“Easy!” Rodjer exclaimed and looked at Juniper in surprise. “You don’t really mean to say . . . that you think all this . . . is easy for me.” 
“No. I didn’t mean easy that way.” Juniper watched Rodjer’s face carefully. “I meant . . . easy in avoiding talking to me by running out. We’ve got to talk about all this sooner or later. And . . . I vote for sooner.” She looked at him, a bit of a smile on her face. “If you think all this is not easy for you . . . what about me? I’ve read all the reports. I know about why some people buy AIs. I know exactly what I’m missing.” Her smile got a tad bigger. “Well . . . maybe I don’t know exactly what I’m missing . . . but I’ve got a pretty good idea.” 
Juniper took Rodjer’s hand and began to walk to the door. “So . . . we’re going to cool off. Then . . . we’re going to talk.” She paused at the door and looked up at him very seriously. “I’m going to take this blanket off . . . and we’re going to go swimming. Then we’re going to come back in here and drink gallons of coffee and talk all night.” 
Rodjer didn’t say a word. He just watched in silence. He watched in silence as Juniper walked out the door. He watched in silence as she let the blanket slip from her shoulders and fall to the ground outside. He watched in silence as she walked towards the beach. He watched in silence as the waves slowly rose to cover her body as she entered the water. 
It was a few minutes before Rodjer trusted himself to move. Then he went and retrieved the blanket and shook it off and folded it and put it inside the dome. He took off his shorts and t-shirt, putting them on top of the blanket. And then he walked slowly towards the beach. 
Juniper bobbed up and down with the waves as she watched Rodjer come towards her over the sand. The moonlight was bright enough so that she could see every detail. She wondered how the hell she was going to keep her hands off of him until morning.
She had had her share of male friends. She just hadn’t liked any of them well enough to venture out into space with them. Besides, she had researched AIs and was fascinated with the possibilities that existed in a human-AI relationship. She just wanted to make damn sure that if she and Rodjer ever made love, she would be making love with a being that possessed free will. Not just a being programmed to do this or that. So she watched Rodjer come across the sand towards her and repeated to herself again and again “not tonight”. 
By mutual unspoken agreement, Juniper and Rodjer swam at some distance from each other. He body-surfed a few of the small waves onto the beach and she either swam in place watching him or floated on her back in the cool water beyond the breakers watching the moon and stars displayed in the sky above her. 
After a bit Juniper went back to the Beach Shack. She showered off and toweled off and then borrowed Rodjer’s shirt to wear. It didn’t cover her completely, but at least the area from chest to mid-thigh was reasonably well covered. All she had to do was not be too careless where and how she put her legs. 
Juniper had just requested a mug of hot coffee with a bit of coffee liquor in it and a scoop of chocolate ice cream floating on top, when Rodjer entered. He had showered and he had one towel wrapped around his waist and he was using another towel to dry his hair. Rodjer gave Juniper a slightly sheepish look and retrieved his shorts, disappearing into the bathroom. A few moments later he reemerged with his hair hurriedly brushed and his body semi-clothed. 
“What are you drinking?” Rodjer asked as he headed towards the Food Dispenser.
“I’m having coffee . . . with a nip of coffee liquor in it and a bit of chocolate ice cream floating on top.” Juniper answered from where she sat on the bearskin rug. She had turned the fire up just a tad and the warmth of it felt good after her swim. 
“That sounds good . . . I think I’ll have one, too.” Rodjer completed his request at the Food Dispenser and turned to face Juniper holding his drink. “Where shall I sit?” 
“Right here by me.” Juniper patted the rug by her side. “I promise to keep my hands to myself.” She smiled at him. 
“Well . . . I make no promises.” Rodjer eyed Juniper’s legs where the shirt ceased to cover their length. “But . . . I’ll try.” 
“Good.” Juniper said as he sat down a little ways away from her. “Now . . . where shall we start?” 
“If you don’t mind . . . could we start with why you had me detail that picture in my head while I was buried in the ice?” Rodjer began to sip at his drink. 
“Sure, that’s as good a place as any to start. It was just my attempt to help you stay warm. Years ago Laurel did some biofeedback. She was able to warm her hands up. She could make her hands go from seventy-four degrees to ninety-eight degrees in about half an hour. I thought it was worth a try. And it couldn’t hurt. At the very least, your mind would be occupied until we got there. And there was a chance that it would even warm you up a bit. So I tried.” Juniper sounded a bit proud of herself. 
“I knew if I asked you . . . you’d come up with a plausible explanation for your request. I don’t know if it made me any warmer, I’ll have to check with Amelia and see.” Rodjer grinned at Juniper. “But . . . I guarantee you . . . it did occupy my mind.” 
“Yes . . . I guess I just heard proof of that.” Juniper grinned back. “It heated me up . . . just with you telling me about it. It will be interesting to see what information Amelia can give us. If your picture could heat me up just hearing about it, maybe it did do some good for you to picture it down there.” 
“Well, in the morning, we’ll check with her.” Rodjer paused for a moment. “I suppose . . . now . . . you want to talk to me about something else.” Rodjer had turned expectantly to face Juniper. 
“Yes . . . where to start? Well . . . I’ll start at the beginning. I designed you with Rowan and Laurel’s help and all the latest advancements to be as human as possible.” Juniper paused. “But . . . you know all that. We attempted to instill in you as much freedom of choice as we could. And lately we’ve been working on common sense. But . . . you know all this.” She paused again. “I’m not doing this very well. All I’m doing is saying what you already know.” 
“Let me see . . . if I can cut through to the basics of what you’re trying to say.” Rodjer looked at Juniper seriously. “If I offend you . . . or upset you . . . when I say this . . . please let me know. I’ll stop immediately. I don’t want to upset you.” 
“It’s sweet of you to be so concerned about my feelings. We tried very hard to give you a great deal of freedom in your programming, to be able to decide you don’t want to be around me or owned by me any more. We wanted you to have the freedom to leave the spaceship and make your own way with our help, if you needed it, should you decide you don’t want to live with us anymore.” Juniper tried to explain. 
“I’ve never understood that. Why would you go to such expense and time and effort to purchase and create me and then give me the freedom to leave? It doesn’t make much sense and it’s not very efficient.” Rodjer seemed genuinely puzzled. 
“I want you to be here of your own free will. We don’t want anyone around us . . . not you, not Maynard, not any AI, or any human either . . . unless they want to be here. And the reason is quite simple. We want to know that those who share our lives want to share them.” Juniper attempted to help Rodjer understand. 
“I can’t believe that you and Rowan and Laurel wouldn’t be upset, if Maynard or I left. It always seems like you all care a great deal about us . . . giving us the latest updates, the ability to taste, and other programs that you’ve designed especially for us.” Rodjer struggled to understand what Juniper was trying to tell him. 
“Oh . . . we’d be very upset if you or Maynard left. But we’d still want you to go, if you felt you needed or wanted to leave us. The belief that every being should have that freedom is very strong in us. Also it’s important to us that we know for sure that those who live and work with us, want to be with us. That’s definitely a must.” Juniper was as emphatic as she could be. 
“I still don’t really understand the reasoning behind what you’re saying. But I understand that in some way the knowledge that a being is choosing of free will to be here is much more important to you than the pain you would suffer if that being decided to leave.” Rodjer was beginning to understand. Though he had to admit, the concept of free choice must be very important to them. 
“You’re getting the idea now.” Juniper confirmed his observations.
“So . . . you’re saying that you’d rather I left and went away . . . than stay if I didn’t want to.” Rodjer watched Juniper’s face very carefully. 
“Yes. It would hurt me deeply. More deeply than I know I’m aware of now and more deeply than I could probably stand. But yes . . . if you didn’t want to be with me . . . then I would want you to go.” Juniper steadfastly held Rodjer’s gaze, but she felt a small tear attempting to leak out of her eyes at just the thought of him wanting to leave her.
“The more I’m around humans the more they amaze me. You’ll spend money, time, and effort creating something and then turn around and give it the freedom to leave if it wants. Viewed from the purely efficient point of view, it doesn’t seem very efficient.” Rodjer couldn’t understand the risk versus benefit ratio.
“Oh . . . but it is efficient. If it works . . . if you do create a being and it does decide to stay . . . the benefit is very much worth any risk taken. It’s sort of an ‘all or nothing’ point of view.” Juniper tried to clarify what she meant a little further. 
“I see. The risk is very high but the benefit is very high as well. OK . . . maybe I am beginning to see the light.” Rodjer looked at Juniper and reached out and touched her hand. 
“So . . . Junie . . . can you explain to me . . . why you risked your life to rescue me down on the comet’s surface?” Rodjer asked quietly.
“Well . . . it’s very simple why Rowan and Laurel risked their lives. I was going down there to try to rescue you. No one could’ve stopped me. They realized this and didn’t try to stop me. They also knew that if I went down there alone and attempted a rescue, I’d fail at best and at worst I’d probably die. There was just no way they were going to let me go down there alone and they knew they couldn’t keep me from going.” Juniper knew what was coming. 
“That explains why they risked their lives. But why . . . did you risk yours? You must have realized that not only were you risking your life, you were risking their lives as well. You must have realized that if you insisted on going, they wouldn’t let you go alone. You knew you would be risking their lives and yours. Why would you do a thing like that? It was all so needless. You could always have built another AI.” Rodjer searched Juniper’s face for the answer. 
“Well . . . I’m not sure I’m ready for you to know the answer to that question. But . . . I can give you part of the answer. I just hope to God . . . it’ll be enough to satisfy you.” Juniper paused and gazed into the fire. She watched the flames dance and felt her face flush as she remembered the picture Rodjer had conjured up under the ice. 
“This may be difficult for you to understand . . . but if you had died on the comet and we had built another AI and made it look just like you . . . it wouldn’t have been you. We could have made it just like we made you. We could have included all the updates, your memory, everything we could, and it still wouldn’t have been you.” Juniper paused and turned to face Rodjer. “I would’ve had to teach the new AI all over again how to boogie board. It would have had all the information on what one would need to know to do it, but it wouldn’t know how to do it. It wouldn’t know how to feel the waves. That’s the only way I can think of . . . to explain the difference.” Juniper paused and looked away again, losing herself in the flames that flitted and spat in the fireplace. “And that difference was important enough to me . . . to risk my life and the lives of my parents. I just didn’t want the person I’ve experienced all those things with . . . all the things I’ve experienced with you . . . to die. It’s as simple as that. I didn’t want you to die.” 
“But why? Why was I that important? So important that you risked all you did to save me? Even if I can understand that there would be a difference between me and another AI just like me . . . and I can almost get an inkling of what you mean . . . it still doesn’t explain . . . why you should take the risk to save me.” Rodjer’s eyes seemed to be boring into hers in his fierce attempt to understand. 
“That’s the part I don’t want to explain now. Please . . . can’t we just leave it . . . that for whatever bizarre reason . . . it was worth it for me to risk my life and my parents’ lives just to save you. That particular entity that makes up you . . . the you sitting in front of me now . . . those components, those circuits, those eyes . . . the you that has shared so much of my time. It was worth it for me to risk anything . . . to save that you.” Juniper looked away then. She knew the answer must be written across her forehead in bright neon letters. She couldn’t understand why she couldn’t tell Rodjer . . . she just wasn’t brave enough yet. Hopefully soon she would be. 
“OK, we’ll leave it at that . . . for now. And I think I can pretty much understand it . . . up to that point. There maybe a difference between me and another AI made in my image. I’m just beginning to understand what you mean by the possible differences there might be. And maybe soon . . . you’ll be able to help me understand the rest . . . the last piece of the puzzle.” Rodjer reached out and gently turned Juniper’s face so his eyes met hers. 
“Now . . . we probably should talk about that other matter . . . that filled this room to overflowing right before we escaped and went swimming tonight.” Rodjer said quietly. 
Juniper struggled to meet Rodjer’s gaze. She had a sudden desperate urge to refill her coffee cup or go to the bathroom or look at the fire . . . do anything . . . but sit here and look at him and talk about what they had to talk about. 
“You’re right . . . we’ve got to talk about that. But . . . I’m not sure . . . I can talk to you . . . and look at you, too.” Juniper replied slowly. 
“That ugly . . . am I? You just can’t bear . . . to look at me?” Rodjer grinned down at her. 
“Just the opposite . . . and you know it.” Juniper reached up and out with her arm, faking a punch to his beautifully formed nose. 
She pulled back just before she made contact with his nose. As Juniper was pulling her fist back, Rodjer caught it and held it.
Rodjer brought Juniper’s fist towards him. One by one he slowly uncurled her fingers and kissed the tip of each of them. When he had finished kissing her fingertips, he kissed the palm of her hand and then raised his eyes to where she was watching him. Rodjer smiled then, just barely letting a smile light up his eyes and Juniper found herself smiling back just a little. 
He hoped to God he knew what he was doing, because for once in his life he did something without thinking. He slowly leaned forward and ever so gently kissed Juniper’s lips, and then just as slowly he leaned back and searched her face for any sign of whether he knew what he was doing or not. Juniper didn’t appear to be reacting to his kiss at all and then gradually a small tear made its way down her cheek, just one small tear and that was all. 
When he saw the tear, Rodjer felt his insides tighten in a way he hadn’t experienced before. The one and only time he had risked action before thought, he had made Juniper cry. He shouldn’t have trusted himself. He should have thought first. 
“I’m sorry . . . Junie . . . I should have thought first. I never do anything without thinking. I didn’t mean to make you cr . . .” Juniper pressed a finger tip gently on Rodjer’s lips to silence him. 
“It’s all right, Rodjer. I’m just . . . incredibly happy. Sometimes when I’m really happy my eyes kind of well up.” Juniper spoke softly. 
“You cry . . . when you’re happy?” Rodjer sounded surprised, but relieved. “I didn’t know that.” 
“Oh . . . there’re lots of things about me . . . you don’t know . . . yet.” A smile lit Juniper’s eyes, and then her face grew serious. “But . . . first things first . . . we have to have that talk.” She paused and looked carefully at him. “Now . . . I think I’ll just get that pillow off the bed . . . and go over there . . . and lean up against the bed . . . and talk to the back of your head.” Juniper jumped up before Rodjer could answer and got the pillow and arranged herself on the floor with her back against the bed and Rodjer about four feet in front of her. 
“You’re going to have to sit and face the fireplace . . . if this is going to work.” Juniper said, for he had turned to watch her and no longer had his back to her. 
“You want me to face the fireplace . . . so all you see is the back of my head?” Rodjer’s voice was filled with bewilderment. 
“Yes . . . I know it sounds silly. But . . . even if you do that . . . it will be a wonder if I can get out what I need to say.” Juniper calmly waited while Rodjer turned around. “Now . . . stay like that until I say you can look at me . . . OK?” 
Rodjer almost turned around to see if Juniper was in her right mind, but he thought better of it. 
“OK . . . if you want me to sit with my back to you and talk to the fireplace . . . I will. But don’t ask me to understand why.” Rodjer said in a puzzled voice.
“You don’t have to understand why . . . just . . . please . . . do it.” Juniper pleaded. 
“OK . . . I’ll stay this way . . . until you tell me differently.” Rodjer paused. “Did anyone ever tell you . . . that you’re weird sometimes?” He couldn’t resist adding. 
“Yes . . . they have. And so what? Everyone is weird sometimes. Now . . . please . . . this is hard enough . . . without you commenting on how weird I am.” Juniper knew she was delaying the inevitable. 
The resulting continuing silence was so lengthy that Rodjer finally spoke up. 
“Did you sneak out . . . and leave me here to look at the fire all night . . . waiting for you to say something?” He asked quietly. 
“No . . . I’m still here . . . I’m just trying to get my courage up.” Juniper took a deep breath and plunged ahead. “If I understand what was happening tonight . . . what was going on tonight before we swam in the ocean . . . you wanted to make love to me . . . right?” If she was going to plunge in, she might as well go for the glory. 
“Ah . . . well . . . yes. I wanted to use . . . every talent I had . . . and let me tell you I have a few . . . to convince you to let me get you in bed under the covers . . . or to get you horizontal on the bearskin rug . . . or any other such situation to your liking.” Rodjer seemed to be doing better after a bit of a slow start.
“A simple ‘yes’ would have been sufficient. But . . . I’ll take all that for a ‘yes’.” Juniper paused. “And I must admit . . . that I wanted very much . . . to have my way with you.” Even in the strange predicament in which Juniper found herself, a slight smile tugged at her mouth at the words she had chosen to use. 
“What? You did?” Rodjer caught himself just before he turned around. “Then . . . why didn’t you say something? I was afraid I would upset you . . . if I had read the signals wrong. After all, you haven’t requested such services before. I felt sure that you would ask . . . if you wanted me to make love to you.” 
“Of course I wanted to . . . as much . . . possibly more . . . than you did.” She paused. “I’m not sure how you work . . . I’ll have to ask Amelia . . . or I could ask you. But . . . I definitely crave and need such attentions.” Juniper tried to go on. 
“Why didn’t you say so? Or . . . I guess I could have asked. God . . . I could’ve helped you anytime.” Rodjer began to turn around. “We can do it now.” 
“Don’t you dare move!” Juniper sputtered. “If you so much as turn around . . . I’m out that door. And I don’t care how fast your AI legs are . . . you’ll never catch me.” 
“But . . . you said you wanted to do it.” Rodjer was totally confused now. “Why do you get upset when I suggest we do it?” 
“I don’t want . . . to just do it. I don’t want you to help me. I don’t want your services. I don’t want any of that . . . never . . . ever.” Juniper’s voice had risen just a tad. 
“OK . . . I won’t help you. But what do you want? I don’t understand . . . what you’re trying to say.” Rodjer was not sure what he should do or say. 
“Well . . . I told you . . . I wasn’t very good at this. I created you . . . I made you attractive. It seemed pointless at the time to make an ugly AI. I’ve made you as human as possible . . . with free choice . . . and common sense.” She paused. “I didn’t create you to be my bedmate . . . even though I’ve worked very hard to insure that you’re capable of as much as possible in that regard.” Juniper paused again and Rodjer interrupted her. 
“I thank you very much for the talents you’ve bestowed on me. But . . . I don’t understand why you’ve insured that I have these talents . . . if you don’t want me to use them with you.” Rodjer paused. “But . . . I’m sure you’ll get to that part.” Rodjer’s sense of humor was returning.
“Right . . . well . . . as I was saying before you interrupted . . . I gave you the capabilities . . . but I didn’t create you for a bedmate.” Juniper paused and shifted her position slightly. “Now . . . we come to the uncomfortable part. But . . . here goes . . .” She paused again. “Please . . . please . . . don’t turn around.” Juniper paused to take a deep breath.
“I’m becoming more and more attracted to you. You’re gorgeous. I like being around you . . . and it’s getting to the place . . . like tonight . . . when it’s all I can do to keep my hands off of you. Please . . . don’t say anything.” Juniper begged. She was afraid Rodjer would interrupt again and she was having a hard enough time telling him this as it was.
“The problem that I’m faced with is this. I don’t want to have you service me . . . or help me. I want you to want to make love to me . . . as much as I want to make love to you. I want your decision to make love to me . . . to be based on free choice.” Juniper paused. “I’m very serious. If you ever make love to me . . . I want it to be because it’s your free choice to do so. I don’t want you to do it because you feel like you have to, that it’s your job.” Juniper paused again and a few minutes passed before she went on. “Now . . . I just got done saying that I want you to make love to me. You’ll take that as a request for your services. And I’ll never know . . . if you really want me or if you’re just servicing me.” 
“I see what you mean.” Rodjer said after Juniper remained silent for a few moments. “You want me and you want me to know you want me . . . so I won’t be afraid I’ll upset you if I make advances. But at the same time . . . even though you want me to know you want me . . . you don’t want me to feel obligated to perform. Unless . . . of course . . . I really want to make love to you . . . and then it’s OK.” Rodjer paused. “Do I have this right?” Rodjer couldn’t help it, the smile on his face showed in his words a little. 
“Are you laughing at me?” Juniper was quick to pick up on it.
“No. It’s just a bit of a humorous situation for you to be in . . . and me to be in.” Rodjer was quick to add. “Here we seem to have a healthy female of the human species . . . and a healthy male of the AI species . . . that are trying to make sure that each knows what the other wants.” 
“So . . . what do we do?” Juniper said a bit pitifully. “I want you to know that I want to make love to you . . . but once I let you know that . . . I’ll think that if you make love to me . . . you’re only doing it because you know I want you.” 
“Or because it feels good.” Rodjer piped up. “I do enjoy it, too. Even when I service someone at their request . . . I’d still enjoy it immensely. Believe me . . . it wouldn’t be a hardship on me.” Rodjer started to say something as a smile lit his eyes, but he changed his mind. Juniper’s sense of humor wasn’t what it usually was tonight. 
“Right . . . I know that. But there have also been times in the past . . . like tonight when you wanted me. But you couldn’t say anything . . . because you felt you might offend me.” Juniper was not at all sure she could make Rodjer understand. 
“Well . . . we no longer have that problem. I know now . . . that you want me.” Rodjer attempted in his own way to clear up the problem. 
“Right . . . we solved that part of it. But what about you feeling that since you know I want you . . . you have to do it or it’s your job to do it?” Juniper asked him. 
“Couldn’t I just say . . . ‘I want you, too’ . . . and leave it at that?” Rodjer asked quietly. 
“But how would I know you were saying that out of free choice . . . and not out of some misplaced obligation to fulfill your duty?” Juniper didn’t know how they were ever going to solve this. 
“You could just believe me . . . believe that I wouldn’t lie to you. Take my word for the fact that I honestly . . . from a position of free choice . . . want to make love to you.” A smile came back into Rodjer’s words. “Or . . . I could just refuse your advances for a while . . . until you finally realized I have free choice . . . and then I could make love to you.” 
“I don’t know what to do.” Juniper crawled up on the bed and hugged the pillow to her chest. “You know now . . . how I feel . . . and I’ll never know for sure why you’re doing it . . . if you do . . . do it.” She paused as her voice grew fainter and fainter as it became muffled by the pillow. “Maybe . . . I shouldn’t . . . have said anything.”
Slowly Rodjer turned around and looked at Juniper. She was so huddled around the pillow that she didn’t notice he had turned around. He got to his feet and quietly made his way over to the bed and sat down beside her. He watched as her body tensed when she realized that he was no longer sitting on the floor with his back to her as he was supposed to be. Rodjer gently reached out and brushed a bit of hair from the front of her face. She flinched at his touch.
“Junie . . . I want you to sit up and look at me.” Juniper ignored his words and hugged her pillow closer. “Junie . . . I mean it. Sit up and look at me.” Rodjer paused. “You had your say . . . and made me sit facing the fireplace. Now . . . it’s my turn. I want you to sit up and look at me.” 
“But . . . you didn’t stay sitting like you were supposed to. You’re up here now . . . facing me.” Juniper complained in a muffled voice from the depths of her pillow. 
“I know. But your way seemed to have hit a standstill. Now . . . it’s my turn. And I insist . . . that you sit up and look at me.” Rodjer’s voice was firm. “It’s OK . . . if you want some time to think about it . . . I can sit here all night and wait. But I’m going to sit right here . . . until you sit up and look at me.” Rodjer continued to insist. 
Juniper knew she couldn’t win. He was an AI. He could sit there forever and wait. Maybe she should do what he wanted and get it over with. But she just hugged her pillow and a tear or two slid down her face. 
“Junie . . . come on. I’m sorry you’re crying. I have a feeling you’re not happy . . . this time. But . . . this is important.” Rodjer’s voice pleaded and it grew ever softer as he spoke. “Junie . . . please sit up and look at me.” 
Quietly without a word, Juniper sat up. She sniffled once and brushed her messy hair out of her eyes. With as much bravery as she could muster, she looked into his eyes. His eyes were filled with concern.
“Thank you . . . Junie.” Rodjer spoke softly. “I know that was hard for you to do.”
Juniper didn’t answer. It was all she could do to keep on looking Rodjer in the eyes. She felt his hand find hers among the covers, and she surprised herself at how hard she hung onto his hand once it found hers. 
“Junie . . . I don’t want to have to reject you . . . just so you can be sure that the decisions that I make are my own.” Rodjer spoke softly and firmly. “You’re going to have to trust me. That’s the only other choice we have.” 
“I . . . know.” Juniper mumbled quietly. 
“Good. Now that you realize we only have two choices . . . we can go on.” Rodjer paused and wiped a tear from where it had settled on Juniper’s cheek. “Now . . . I need to know what I can do or say that will help you to believe that my decisions are my own.”
“But . . . if I tell you what you can do or say to help me believe you . . . you’d just be doing those things to help me believe . . . not because you wanted to do them.” Juniper managed to point out. 
“You’re right. This is going to be harder that I thought.” Rodjer sighed. “Well . . . I’ll just have to do the best I can. I’m a bit new at this . . . but I’m sure something will help you to believe. Since I really am making my own decisions . . . sooner or later something I do will help you believe.”
“In the meantime . . . what do we do?” Juniper mumbled through her pillow. She wasn’t looking at Rodjer and her face was once again half buried in the pillow. 
“Junie . . . look at me.” Rodjer waited while Juniper lowered the pillow and slowly raised her eyes to meet his. 
“Junie . . . you and I have been through a lot . . . right?” Rodjer waited until he got a small nod in return. “So . . . we’ll make it through this. We’ll figure it out together . . . OK?” 
Once again Juniper managed a small nod. 
“But . . . what do we do in the meantime?” She asked in a small voice.
“Well . . . we do what we want to do. You let me know what you want . . . and I let you know what I want.” Rodjer paused and slowly tilted Juniper’s chin up. “And you believe that what I’m doing . . . is what I want to do.” He watched her face carefully. “I disobeyed your order to remain sitting facing the fireplace. I insisted that you sit up and look at me . . . even when you were crying and you didn’t want to do what I asked. So you can tell . . . I’m making my own decisions.” 
Juniper slowly nodded her head in the affirmative. 
“But . . . what do we do now?” She plaintively asked again.
“What do you want to do?” Rodjer asked reaching out to brush a bit of her hair out of her face. “I’ve a suggestion as to what I’d like to do.” 
“What?” Juniper mumbled. 
“I’d like to sit right over there behind you and hold you while you sleep a bit. You could get a bit of rest and I could wake you right before the sunrise. Then we could watch the sunrise together and decide what we want to eat for breakfast. How does that sound to you?” Rodjer smiled into Juniper’s eyes and she even managed a small smile in return. 
“I’d like that.” She answered quietly. 
“OK . . . consider it done.” Rodjer replied softly. 
And without another word, Rodjer got up and climbed into bed behind Juniper. Leaning his back against the cushions piled at the head of the bed, he pulled her towards him and settled her under the covers on his lap. He carefully tucked the covers up under her chin. She squirmed a bit until she got settled comfortably in his arms. He watched as she closed her eyes. Gently and ever so lightly, he brushed the hair from her forehead and the sides of her face. He continued to stroke her hair slowly until he could tell by her breathing that she was asleep. Then he settled back to review all that had been said and done that night while he waited for the sun to rise. 

* * *
